MINISTRY OF PERSONNEL & TRAINING, 
ADMINISTRATIVE REFORMS AND PUBLIC 
GRIEVANCES AND PENSION 
(Department of Pension and Pensioner's Welfare) 

New Delhi, the 17th April, 1985 

S.O. 1870.—In exercise of the powers conferred by the 
proviso to article 309 read with clause (5) of aiticle 148 of 
the Constitution of Tndia and after consultation with the 
Comptroller and Auditor General in relation to persons 
serving; in the Indian ^.udit and Accounts Department, the 
President hereby makes the following rules further to amend 
the Central Civil Service® (Commutation of Pension) Rules. 
1981, namely :— 

1. (i) These rules may be called the Cential Civil Seivices 
(Commutation of Pension) (Amendment) Rules, 1985. 


(ii) They shall come into force on the date of (heir 
publication in the Gazette of India. 

1. in the Central Civil Services (Commutation 0 f Pension) 
Rules, 1981, (i) for rllle 7, the folio whig shall be substituted, 
namely : — 


"7. ’Nominations 

(1) An applicant shall make a nomination in Foim 5 
alongwith the application referred to in rule 13 or 
rule 19, as the case may be, conferring on one or 
more persons the right to receive the commuted 
value of pension in casle the applicant dies without 
receiving the commuted value on or after the date 
on which commutation became absolute. 


(2) If there is no such nomination, or if the nomination made does not subsist, the commuted value shall be paid to the family 

in the manner indicated in sub-rule (1) (b) of Rule 51 of the Pension Rules. 

(3) If in any case the commuted value cannot be paid in the manner indicated in sub-rules (1) and (2), the same shall be paid 

to his heirs” 

(ii) after Form 4, the following Form shall be inserted, namely:— 


"(FORM 5) 
(See Rule 7) 


To

(Place).

granted an Import Licence No. P/F/2030105/C|XX|83|H|83 
dated 5-10-82 fo r Rs. 29,67,974 only foi the import of spares. 

2. The firm have, now requested for issue of Duplicate 
Exchange Control Copy of the above licence on the ground 
that the original Exchange Control Copy has been lost/ 
misplaced having being registered with the Custom Authority, 
Bombay and utilised the same to the extent of 
Rs. 18,06,852 only. Now the firm agrees and undertakes 
to leturn the original exchange control copy, if traced later 
on to this office for record. 

3, In support of thei r contention, M/s. Mekaster Trading 
Corpn. New Delhi have filed an affidavit as required in 
terms of para 353 of Chapter XV of Hand Dook of Import- 
Export procedures fo r 1984-85. The undersigned is satisfied 
that the original Exchange Control Copy of Import Licence 
No. P|F|2030I05 dated 5-10-82 has been lost and directs 
that duplicate exchange control copy may be issued in 
the applicant. The original exchange control copy is hereby 
treated as cancelled. 


4. The duplicate Exchange_ Control Copy of the Import 
Licence is being issued separately.

S.O. 1893.—^Whereas it appears to the Central Government 
that it is necessary in the public interest that for the trans¬ 
port ol petroleum from Bijaipur (M.P.) to Sawai Madhopnr 
in Rajasthan State pipeline should be laid by the Oil and 
Natural Gas Commission; 

And whereas it appeals that for the puipose of laying such 
pipeline, it is necessary to acquire the right of user in the 
land described in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in the Land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares 
its intention to acquire the tight of user therein; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification object, to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Authority, Oil & Natuial Gas Commiss ; on, Constiuction and 
Maintenance Division, H.B.T. Gas Pipeline Project, 49, Indra 
Colonv, Sawai Madhopnr. 

And every person making such an objection shall also state 
specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person or by 
legal practitioner.

S.O. 1897.—Whereas It appears to the Central Government 
that it is nccessaiy in the public interest that for the trans¬ 
port of petroleum from Bijaipur (M.P.l to Sawni Madhopur 
in Rajasthan State pipeline should be laid by the Gas Autho¬ 
rity of India I imited; 


And whereas it appears that for the pin pose of laying such 
pipeline, it is necessary to acquire the right of user in the 
land described in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, m exercise of powers conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in the Land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962j, the Central Government hereby declares 
its intention to acquire the right of user therein ; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification, object to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Authority, 031 and Natural Gfts Commission, Construction 
and Maintenance Division, H.B.T. Gas Pipeline Project, 49, 
Tndra Colony, Sawai Madhopur.

that it is necessary in the public interest mat lor the trans¬ 
port of petroleum from Bijaipur (M.P.) to Sawai Madhopur 
in Rajasthan State pipeline should be laid by the Gas Autho¬ 
rity of India Limited; 

And whereas it appears that lor the purpose of laying 
such pipeline, it is necessary to acquire the right of user m 
the land described in the schedule annexed here'o; 

Now. therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in the Land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares 
its intention to acquire the tight of user therein; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of tins notification, object to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Authority Oil and Natural Gas Commission, Construction 
and Maintenance Division, H.B.J. Gas Pipeline Project, 49,

S.O. 1902.—Whereas it appears to the Central Government 
that it is necessary*'in the public interest that for the transport 
of petroleum from Bijftipur (M.P.) to Sawai Waelhopur in 
Rajasthan State pipeline should be laid hy the Gas Authority 
of India Limited; 

And whereas it appears ih.y for the purpose of laying slk.Ii 
pipeline, it is necessary to acquire the riuht of user in the 
land desenbed in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipeluies (Acquisition of Right of user in th e Lanl) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government declares its 
intention to acquire the right ot user therein; 


Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 2l days from the date of this notification, obje.t to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Aufhority, Oil and Natflral Gas Commission, Construction and 
Maintenance Division. H.B.J. Gas Pipeline Project, 49, Indra 
Colony, Sawai Madhopur. 


And every person making such an objection shall also s’ate 
specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person or by 
legal practitioner.

SO. 1904.—Whereas by notification of the Government 
of India in the Ministry of Energy, Depart¬ 
ment of Petroleum S.O. 978 dated 25-2-85 tinder sub¬ 
section (1) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 19h2), the Central Government declared its intention 
to acquire the right of user in the land specified in the 
Schedule appended to that notification for tho purpose of 
laying pipeline ; 

And whereas the Competent, Authority has under sub¬ 
section (1) of section 6 of the said Act, submitted report 
to the Government ; 

And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report, decided to acquire the right of 
user in the lands specified in the Schedule appended to this 
notification ; 

Now, thercfoie, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (1) of the Section 6 of the said Act, the Central 
Government hereby declares that the right of user in the 
said lands specified in the Schedule appended to this notifica¬ 
tion hereby acquired for laying the pipeline ; 

And further in exercise of power conferred by sub-section 
(4) of that section, the Central Government directs that 
the right of user ;n the said lands shall instead of vesting 
in Central Government vests from this date of the publication 
of this declaration in the Gas Authority of India Ltd. 
free from encumbrances.

S.O. 1906.—Whereas by notification of the Government 
of India in the Ministry of -Energy, Depart¬ 
ment of Petroleum S.O. 980 dated 25-2-85 undor sub¬ 
section (I) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 1962), the Central Government declared its intention 
to acquire the right of user in the land specified in the 
Schedule appended to that notification for the purpose of 
laying pipeline ; 

And whereas the Competent Authority has under sub¬ 
section (1) of section 6 of the said Act, submitted report 
to the Government ; 

And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report, decided to acquire the right of 
user in the lands specified m the Schedule appended to this 
notification ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (1) of the Section 6 of the said Act, the Central 
Go\ eminent hereby declares that the right of user in the 
said lands specified in the Schedule appended to this notifica¬ 
tion hereby acquired for laying the pipeline ; 

And further in exercise of power conferred by sub-section 
(4) of that section, the Central Government directs that 
the right of user in the said lands shall instead of vesting 
in Central Government vests from th\s dftfe of the publication 
of this declaration in the Gas Gas Authority of India Ltd. 
free from encumbrances.

S.Q. 1907.—Whereas by notification of the Government 
of India in the Ministry of Energy, Depart¬ 
ment of Petroleum S.O. 984 dated 25-2-85 under sub¬ 
section (l) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 1962), the Central Government declared its intention 
to acquire the right of user in the land specified in the 
Schedule appended to that notification for the purpose of 
laying pipeline ; 


And whereas the Competent Authority has under sub¬ 
section (1) of section 6 of the said Act, submitted report 
to the Government ; 


And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report, decided to acquire the right of 
user in the lands specified in the Schedule appended to this 
notification ; 


Now', therefoie, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (1) of the Section 6 of thj said Aot t the Central 
Government hereby declares that the right of user in the 
•aid lands specified in the Schedule appended to this not'fica- 
tion hereb\ acquiied for laying the pipeline ; 


And further in exercise of power conferred by sub-iectioi 
(4) of that section, the Central Government direct* that 
the right of user in Ihe said lands shall instead of vesting 
in Central Government vests from this date of the publication 
of this declaration in the Grs Authority of India Ltd. 
free from encumbrances.

S.O. 1908.—Whereas by notification of the Government 
of India in the Ministry of Energy, Depart¬ 
ment of Petroleum S.O. 982 dated 25-2-85 under sub¬ 
section (0 of Section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 1962), the Central Government declared its intention 
to acquire the right of user ill the land specified in the 
Schedule appended to that notification for the purpose of 
laying pipeline ; 

And whereas the Competent Authority has under sub¬ 
section (1) of section 6 of the said Act, submitted report 
to the Government ; 

And further whereas the Central Government has, after 
considering the said report, decided to acquire the right of 
user in the lands specified- in the Schedule appended to this 
notification ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (1) of the Section 6 of the said Acl, the Central 
Government hereby declares that the right of user in the 
said lands specified in the Schedule appended to this notifica¬ 
tion hereby acquired for laying the pipeline : 

And further in exercise of power conferred by sub-sectioa 
(4) of that section, the Central Oor/emmcnt directs that 
the right of user in the said lands shall instead of vesting 
in Central Government vests from this date of the publication 
of this declaration in the Gas Authority of India Eld. 
free from encumbrances,

New Delhi, the 23rd April, 1985 

S O. 1915.—Tile following draft of (he Scheme further 
to amend certain Dock Workers (Regulation of Employment) 
Schemes, which the Central Government proposes to make in 
exercise of the powers conferred by (uh-Hcti( - (l)oJ Se t tic r 4 rt 
tkc Dock Workers (Regulation ol Employment) Acl, 1948 (9 of 
1948), is hereby published as required by the said sub-scclion 
for the information of all persons likely to be aflected thereby; 
and notice is hereby given that the said draft will lie taken into 
considcrationonoraftcrtheexpiry of a period oi two months from 
thedate of publication ol this notification ijitbc Officii 1 Gazette.

l.D. No. 8811983 

In the matter of dispute between : 

$|Sri Om Narain Misra, Girish Dutt Shukla, Udni 
Narain Misra, Ashok Kumar Pande, and Shri San¬ 
tosh Kumar Mishra, All C|o Shri O. P. Nis an1 ' 
295J387, Dindayal Road Asharfabad, Kanpur. 

AND 

The Chief Manager, Central Bank of India, Pandu 
Nagar, Kanpur. 

PRESENT : 

Shri O. P. Nigam leprcsentative for the workmen and 
Sii J. Trivedi representative for ihe management. 

AWARD 

The Central Government, Ministry of Labour vide order 
Nos. L-12012] 176|81 .D-H-A, dated 19-12-82 and Order No. 
‘L-12012|191|8I.D-1I dated 26th March, 1982 has refened 
the following disputes for adjudication : 

In the case of l.D. No. 85)1983 : 

“Whether the action of the management of Central 
Bunk of India, Kanpur, in not absorbing Shri Ayo¬ 
dhya Prasad, Sub-Staff in the bank’s services and 
terminating hie seiviccs from 1976 is justified ? If 
not, to what relief is the workmen concerned en¬ 
titled ?’* 

In the case of ID. No. 8811983 : 

“Whether the action of the management of Central 
Bank of Tndia, Kanpur in not absorbing SlShri Om 
Narain Mishra, Girish Dutt Shukla, Udai Narain 
Mishra, Ashok Kumar Pande and Shri Santosh Ku¬ 
mar Mishra, in bank’s services and terminating 
their services from 1976 is justified 7 If not, to 
what relief arc the concerned woikmcn entitled?” 

■/he above two cases were'consolidated as common question 
of law and fact was involved in both the disputes and the 
l.D. No. 85|83 was made leading case and the entire evidence 
of the parties in both the'cases have been adduced in l.D, 
No. 85183. 

It is common ground that the workmen S]Shfi Ayodhya 
Piasad, Om Narain Misra, Girish Dutt Shukla, Udain NaraiVi 
Mishra, Ashok Kumar Pande and Shri Santosh Kumar 
Mishra, worked in the management bunk in subordinate 
cadre at different branches in Kanpur Division. The appli¬ 
cants appeared in a'written test held on 13-6-71 and after 
interview a penal was drawn for the post of sub cadre in 
the bank but the applicants could not qualify the wiitten 
test and interview, hence their names did not apneir in the 
penal list dated 1-8-1971. On the intervention of the hank 
employees Union, the workmen were reinterviewed alongwith 
other candidates and their names were included in the re¬ 
vised penal dated 1-2-73 in the ceneral category. _ It is also 
not disputed that the names of the Udai Narain Misra. 
Om Narain, Girisb Dutt Shukla, Ashok Kumar Pande and 
Sri Santosh Kumar Misra and Avodhya Prasad appeared in 
the merit list of Ihe above said penal at serial Nos. 13, 14, 
16, 21. 26 and 29. According to the hank management this 
merit list remained in operation opto 31st Dccembet. 1973 as 
mentioned therein and the same was scrapped after that date 
Wear the names of the applicants should be considered 



2316 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MAY 4 , I985/VAISAKHA 14, 1907 [Part II— Sec. 3(ii)] 


Further it is not dispute that the applicants have not comp¬ 
leted 240 days of service during span of one year i.e. in a 
12 calendar months preceding the date with reference on 
which calculation is to be made and hence have not acquir¬ 
ed any right. The grievance of the workmen is that the per- 
soas who were below in the merit list get a chance to work 
and were also ullowed to complete 240 days during a year 
which was an unfair labour practice and lastly their absorp¬ 
tion was done permanently by the management bank. In 
this way had the persons below the applicants not allowed 
to complete 240 days and the workmen would have been 
given a chance they would have completed 240 days of work 
in a year and would have been made permanent in the hank 
services. In this way the applicants had to suffer due to 
unfair labour practice on the part of the bank management. 
Despite circular No. 4 of 196$ of the bank outsiders were 
given temporary appointment. The workmen have submitt¬ 
ed the list of 13 persons who were lower to them in the 
merit list of the bank management but were allowed to 
complete 240 day s in span of one year. 

The management had admitted about ten persons that 
their name appeared in the merit list of 1973 and were 
appointed in the bank’s services as they have completed 240 
days or more in one calender month ns temporary workmen 
before their absorption. They are Sri Avadhesh Na'ain 
Pandey, Prcm Chandra, S. K. Savita, M, M. Dsvivedi, K. K. 
Khanna, Dayashankar Dixit, Shiv Shankar Knhar, R. K. 
Sharma and Sri Bidhiram. According to the hank manage¬ 
ment they were at serial nos. '31, 34, 35, 39. 41, 50, 52, 53, 
64 and 66. Most of the above ten persons appointed on 
1-1-72 for a limited period exeept Sri Budhirarn who was 
appointed on 18-5-70. They were allowed to complete 240 
days In one span breaks. No doubt when the revised 
list was made on 1-2-73 and wus valid upto 31-12-73 ns 
mentioned therein. The persons at serial nos. 26, 27 and 
28 were appointed from that list after 31-3-73. 


bank services after having come in the approved list and 
allowed the juniors Shri Avadesh Narain and 9 others to 
complete 240 days. 

In view of the above discussion beliesing tho applicants! 
workmen and evidence on recoid, 1 hold that the action of 
the bank management of Central Bank of India in not ab¬ 
sorbing Shri Ayodhya Prasad of I.D. No, 85 of 83 sub-siaif 
in the bank services and terminating his seivice fiom 1976 
is not justified. 

The icsult is that the applicantlworkman is entitled to be 
reinstate with full back wages 

Similarly the other workmen of T.D, No. N8|83, I hold 
that the action of the management of Central Bank of India 
in not absorbing the workmen namely SiShri Otn, Narain 
Mishra, Girish Dutt Shukla, Udai Narain Mishra, Ashok 
Kumar Pande and Sri Santosh Kumar Mishra in bank ser¬ 
vices and terminating their services from 1976 is not justi¬ 
fied. 

The result is that the applicant]workmen me entitled to be 
reinstatement with full back wages. 

I, therefore, give my award accordingly. 

Let a copy of this) award be kept on tho file of I.D. No. 
88 of 83 Sri Om Narain Mishra and others Vs. Central 
Bank of India.

[No. L-12012!176[81-D.1T(A1] 


The workmen have filed the circular of the Divisional 
Manager dated 13-14 December, 1972. In this circular 
there is a reference that the list of the approved candidates 
in leave vacancy of sub staff dated 31 July, 72 was Issued. 
This circular was the reply of the querrv of some of the 
branches regarding restriction of maximum period of tem¬ 
porary employee could be allowed to work. The reply was 
that the maximum temporary period should be of 80 work¬ 
ing days for approved candidates provided he is working in 
leave vacancy with usual breaks. It was further required 
that the statement showing particulars ("full) of temporary 
91 all; be submitted to the Divisional Office every month from 
5th Deceiriber, 1972. It was further instructed that if an 
approved candidate is working in a clear vacancy he should 
not be allowed 80' working days under anv circumslances and 
the branches should inform immediately if there is any clear 
vacancy and that in no case unapproved should be appoint¬ 
ed. All the workmen in questiofi were getting tempoiary 
appointment uplo 1976 and thus the list prepared on 
1-2-73 did not come to end on 31-12-73 and remained in 
existence upto 1976. 

The workmen representative has laid great stress on circu- 
ler No. 4 of 1968 which laid down also for retrenched cleri¬ 
cal and sub-staff and also mentioned that all further recruit¬ 
ment should be strictly made according to that i ales. The 
circular further laid down that all such vacancies should be 
notified in a local press and thereafter proper applications 
of the candidates not more than five times of the vacancies* 
should he called for the wiitten test. Tt Is also laid down 
in the circular that the temporary workmen should not ty 
allowed to work more than 80 days but the persons jun'or 
to the workmen in the merit list were allowed to continue 
and complete 240 days and were subsequently made per¬ 
manent in the hank service’s. The workman in question 
came in the temporary employment of the hank either in 
1971 or 1972 or latest In 1973 but the bank prepared tbe 
merit list in the year 1973. The services of the applicants 
svere terminated in the vear 1976. Had the applicants 
been allowed to work in place of the persons working in the 
bank who were iunior to the applicants they too might have 
completed 240 davs and would have been made permanent. 
Thus it was malafide on the part of the hank management 
not to have given chance to the workmen to continue in

S.O, 1922,—In pursuance of section 17 of the Indus¬ 
trial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby publishes the award of Central Government Tn- 
distrial Tribunal. Chandigarh, as shown in the Annexure 
in he industrial dispute between the employers in relation to 
the management of Punjab National Bank, Ludhiana, and 
their workmen, wh'ch was received by the Cenlral Govern¬ 
ment on the 8lh April, 1985. 

EFFORT SHRI I. P. VASISHTH, PRESIDING OFFTCFR, 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT, INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL, 
CHANDIGARH. 

Case No. I.D. 142(83 

PARTIES : 

Employers in relation (o the management of Punjab 
National Bank Ludhiana-Punjab. 

AND 

Their workman—Subhash Bansal. 



2317 


[•*rr J r II— 3 (ii)] 


ttr TTnq?r: fff 4, ies sjiwm 14, 19O7 


APPEARANCES ; 

For the Employers.—Shn M'alvinder Singh. 

For the Workman.—S.'Shri V. S. Mu)hi mid T. C- 

Sharma. 

INDUSTRY : Banking. STATE ; Punjab. 

AWARD 

Dated the 29th of March, 1985 

The Central Government, Min stry of Labour, in exercise 
of the powers conferred on them under Section 10(1 )(d) of 
the Itidustnal Disputes Act, 1947, hereinafter referred to as 
the Act, per the Order No. l-UO^/idO/HS-D.ILA. dated 
the 31st of October !9f!3, refcired the following Industrial 
dispute to this Eubunnl lor adjudication : 

“Whethei the action of the management of Punjab 
National Bank, Regional Office, Ludhiana in telation 
to theii Amurgurh Branch in debarring Shri Subhash 
Bansnl. Cleric[C ashier from officiating as well as 
permanent posting as Clerk-cum-Cashier-Incharge 
for y period of one year with effect from 14-12-81 
is justified ? If not to what relief is the woikman 
concerned entiled ?’’. 


5. Resisting the proceedings on all counts, the manage¬ 
ment aNcred that under the terms of Bipartite Settlement 
the petitioner was requited to give an unqualified consent 
for his permanent posting as Cashier-Incharge otherwise ho 
was to loose officiating as well as permanent posting for full 
one year. Although his narration of facts was not contro¬ 
verted yet it was pleaded that his insistance on reserving a 
right to be posted as Teller while officiating as Clerk-cum- 
Cashicr fneharge was quite unwarranted and unreasonable. 
Otherwise also having regard to the nature of their business 
it was necessary to fill up the post of Cushier-lncharge on 
permanent basis. Elaborating the point they propounded 
that it was not possible ior them to keep such a sensative 
post vacant and carry on with make-shift arrangement be¬ 
cause of late there have been enormous increase in the day- 
to-day transactions in the Banking Industry, followed by the 
consequent risk® of cheating and frauds in Cash-section ; 
Moreover the Cashier Tncharge is required to hold joint cus¬ 
tody of the entire Cash ulongwitn the Branch Manager. In 
order to ensure smooth functioning of the Blanch and to 
avoid the hazards of monetary deflections, it had become 
absolutely necessary to fill up the said post on permanent 
basis, at the very first available opportunity. That was how 
that in the terms of Bipartite Settlement the post was offer¬ 
ed to the petitioner, he being the senior most eligible emp¬ 
loyee, but he failed to give liis unconditional consent des¬ 
pite repeated reminders, thus forcing their hands in passing 
the impunged order. 


2. Brief facis of the case, according to the petitioner|work- 
man, are that he was employed as a clerk in the respdt. 
Bank at their Amargarh Branch and was the senio>- most 
person amongst the clerical staff in September 1980. The 
Regional Manager Ludhiana Region had ordered the intro¬ 
duction of Teller-system in the said branch and on account 
of his eligibility, tht petitioner was directed to be posted 
there as Teller under his orders dated 20-10-1980. It was 
alleged that for some oblique reason the Branch Manager 
was not favourably inclined to the proposition and thus, on 
one or the other pretext, he kept on putting off the matter 
which forced the petitioner to raise an industrial dispute 
through his Union. However, the issue could not be settled 
amicably despite the intervent on of the Conciliation machi¬ 
nery and so the Asstt. Labour Commissioner (C) submitted 
a “failure-report" to the Labour Ministry which was still 
seized of the subject for seeking adjudication under Section 
10 of the Act ibid. In between this period the Cashier- 
Incburge of the Bank was transferred and the vacancy was 
proposed to be filled in by the petitioner. 

3. It was against the aforesaid backdrop that under his 
letter dated 1-10-81 the Branch manager sought the peti¬ 
tioner's consent for accepting the permanent posting of 
Cashier-Tnchargc. The petitioner did not oppose the pro¬ 
position but replied that he was willing to officiate os Clerk- 
cum-cashier-Incharge provided it did not effect his claim for 
tl'c post of a Teller for which a dispute was already pending 
bcfoie the Appropriate Government. The Branch Manager 
did not react kindly to the rider and so under his letter 
dated 16-11-81 asked him to give an unconditional acceptance 
or to loose the chance of officiating as well as permanent 
posting for a period of one year in the terms of Bipartite 
Settlement. On th c same day the petitioner informed the 
Branch Manager that he was willing to accept the offer 
without prejudice to his rights on the seat of Teller. On 
2-11-1981 the Brunch Manager again wrote him a similar 
letter which was replied by the petitioner on 11-12-81 in 
reiteration of h s earlier stand. 

4. Tt wav pleaded that the dispute raised by the Petitioner's 
Union on the point of his posting as Teller was negatived by 
the Labour Ministry vide their letter dated 22-4-82 as it 
declined to make any reference under Section 10 of the Act. 
But meanwhile the Respdt, Bank passed the Impunged order 
debarring him from officiating as well as as permanent posting 
as Clcrk-cum-cashier-lncharge for a period of one year w.e.f. 
14-12-81 It was compla ; ned that the managements action 
was highly prejudicial to his right to draw a special allow¬ 
ance of Rs. 1Q2/- A.M. usually allowed to the officiating post 
of Cashier-Incharge and since it was passed in an illegal 
manner, without considering his peculiar circumstances as 
enumerated above so it required to be quashed. 

88 01/85—11 


6. In view of the omnibus nature of the terms of refe¬ 
rence, without going through the formality of framing any 
issue the parties were straightaway called upon to adduce 
evidence in support of their respective versions. According¬ 
ly, the petitioner examined himself on the basis of the 
disclosures made in bis affidavit Ex. W1 whereas, in their 
discretion, the management opted against leading any evi¬ 
dence. They rather tried to meet his case on the basis of 
admitted facts and documents alone. 

7. On a careful scrutiny of the entire available data and 

hearing the parties, I am inclined to sustain thc Manage¬ 
ment’s action in its pith and substance because persual of 
their correspondence, whose authenticity was not disputed 
from either side, right from the initial stage would leave 
no maimer of doubt that the petitioner had throughout been 
insisting on protecting his right to the post of a Teller even 
while working on the promotion seat of Cashier-Incbargc. 
On the other hand, time and again they asked him to recon¬ 
sider his stand and avail of the promotion on giving an un¬ 
conditional consent. It goes without saying that according 
to the common case of the parties the post of a Casbier- 

Tncharge carries a special Allowance and also opens avenues 

of promotion to other senior posts in the Bank, 

8. On behalf of the petitioner it was urged that since his 

claim for being posted as Teller against a vacancy created 
by the Management itself was under consideration with the 
AI.C and subsequently with the Labour Ministry for mak¬ 
ing a regular reference under Section 10 of the Act, there¬ 
fore, he could not possibly forge his right on giving an un¬ 
conditional consent to work as Cashier-Incharge. but even 
then he agreed to discharge the duty of Cashier-Incharge on 
temporary basis while reserving bis right to the Teller’s 

cost. In short, an effect was made to project his bonafides 

in thc refusal. 

9. I am not impressed with the half baked explanation. 
The pertinent point is that the post of Cashier-Incharge in 

a financial institution like Bank, in the very nature of - 
things, requires full time attention by a responsible official 
who should be accountable to the Management for the 
smooth functioning of the Cash Section, and precisely for 
this very reason the management had agreed to give him 
permanent posting on the seat at the very first Instance. Of 
oourse his claim for appointment as Teller was under con¬ 
sideration of the Labour Ministry but that by itself did not 
confer any vested right on him. After all 'he Ministry 
could not "force the,Management to give him any particular 
relief and, in Its discretion, could eveu refuse to make a 
Reference under Section 10 of the Act. And if nt all it 
were to bail him out bv making anv reference to the Tri¬ 
bunal. It was for the latter to decide the cas e on merits 



2318 


THE GA/CElTE OF INDIA : MAY 4, 1985/VAISAKHA 14, 1907 [Pari II— Sec. 3(ii)J 


which could go either way, obviously during all this while 
the Manx uouid not Keep tne post or cmsmei-lncnarge 

vacant. 

10. Looking towards the issue Irom yet another angle, 
suppose (he management were to oiler tne post to the pet- 
son placed immediately below the petitioner and the Minis¬ 
try were to decline the Relerencc, or the Kcterenc proceed¬ 
ings were destined to ta.l betore the Tribunal, what would 
be the fate of that poor chap who was doing the job duti¬ 
fully / Should he oe asked to make loom for the peti¬ 
tioner ? or should the Bank pay Special Allowance lor the 
same post to both of them ! in my considered opinion it 
would be quite an unfair pioposition. After all lor the 
convenience of the petitioner alone, the entire Body con¬ 
sisting of the Management as well as his junior colleagues 
could not be kept in suspencc. Similarly taking the other 
side of it, suppose the Bank had acceeded to the petitioner’s 
request to reserve his right to the post of "I elier, allowed 
him to officiate on the seat of the Cashier-Incharge and 
the Reference were to succeed before the Tribunal; surely 
the petitioner would have an option to grab either of the 
two Allowanc Posts but during all this period at least one 
of his junior colleague would have been deprived of a 
legitimate chance to officiate on an Allowance post. To 
press home a proposition of the type i.e. letting one parti¬ 
cular individual to eat the cake and have it also and dump 
the other into the gutter; from my view point, would be a 
cruel joke on the rules of fair play. 

11. The gist of the matter is that at least up to the po : nt 
of Ministry’s decision on the question of inferring his 
Teller’s dispute to the Tribunal, the petitioner was reluc¬ 
tant to work as Cashier lucharge, and on own showing the 
Ministry declined the Reference on 22-4-1982 whereas the 
vacancy of the Cashier-Incharge arose and was offered to 
him as early as October 1981. 

12. Be that as it may, in Ihe totality of the situation I 
find no irregularity, impropriety or illegality in th c basic 
structure of the impunged order except, of course, that 
instead of 14-12-1981 it should have been effective from 
the very stage when the petitioner failed to give an un¬ 
conditional consent by declining the offer per his letter 
dated 5-10-1981. Therefore, 1 return my Award in favour 
of the Management with such modification in the impunged 
order. 

Chandigarh, 

29-3-1985. 

I. P. VASISHTH, Presiding Officer

REFERENCE : 

Order No. L-12012/300/82-D.H (A), dated 24-5-1983, 
Ministry of Labour and Rehamlitation, Govern¬ 
ment of India, New Delhi. 

This dispute coming on for final hearing on Tuesday, 
the 16th day of October, 1984 upon perusing the reference, 
claim and counter statements and all other material papers 
on records and upon hearing the arguments of Thiruvalargal 
S. Venkalaraman and C. M. Krishnakumar, Advocates ap¬ 
pearing for the workman and of King and Partridge, Advo¬ 
cate appearing l'or thc Management and this dispute having 
stood over till this day for consideration, th s Tribunal made 
the following. 

AWARD 

Thc Central Government by its Order No.-L-DOD^OOISZ-, 
D.II (A), Ministry of Labour and Rehabilitation, dated 24th 
May, 1983 has referred the following dispute for adjudica¬ 
tion to th's Tribunal under Section HKljfd) read with 
Section 7A of the Industrial Disputes Act 1947. 

2. The dispute is : 

Whether the demand of the workmen in relation to the 
Local Head Office of the Slate Bank of India, 
Madras that Shri C. Rajendran. A/C Plant Opera¬ 
tor should be placed in clerical cadre and paid 
appropriate wages and allowances by the Manage¬ 
ment of the Bank if justified ? If not. to what 
relief is the workman concerned entitled ? 

3. On ieceipt of notice from thc Tribunal, ihe Union filed 
a claim statement raising thc following grounds to sustain 
the claim of the worker ; Shri C. Rajendran was appointed 
in the Madras Local Head Office State Bank of India as 
Air Condition Plant Operator on 2nd November, 1977. His 
salary and other service conditions were governed by the 
‘Local Award' read with the subsequent modifications etc. 
made to the tetms of the said award from t'mc to time through 
hi pmtite settlements between the Bank and the All India 
State Bank of India Staff Federation. Shri C. Raiendran 
whs appointed as "Air-Condition Plant Operator" in the scale 
of pay as applicable to the member of the subordinate staff. 
He was also paid a "specul allowance of Rs. 48 per month 
as applicable to the employees in the subordinate cadre per¬ 
forming the duties of "electrician,” The revised scale of 
nay as apnlicable for the subordinate staff in 'A* class is 
245-7-280-19-330-12-390-15-435-20-455, Shri C. Raiendran 
was fitted into this new scale with effect from 1-9-1978. 

4. Shri C Rajendran at the time of his appointment was 
not a matriculate and as such he did not raise any dispute 
regarding thc scale of pay applied to him. Tic. however, 
came to know that employees working in the Central Office 
or the Bank at Bombay, designed as u'r-cnndfiirined plant 
attendants and performing identical duties were appointed in 
the scale of pay applicable to clerical staff and also paid a 
special allowance of Rs. 61 per month plus dearness allo¬ 
wance 1 hereon. From 1-9-197R, the scale of pay applicable 
to the clerical staff in the bank was revised ns : 325-20-405- 
25-455-30-^43-35-580-40-660-45-750-50.800 60.1040. The reJa. 
fir (metini allowance was also revised as Rs. 200 per month. 
Shri C. Bniendran also came to know that his counter-par's 
rt Bombr.v. although matriculates possessed Identical techni¬ 
cal qualification viz., a certificate from the Industrial Training 
Institute tha' 'hey ivere qualified "fitters’’. Shr’ C. Raiendran 
passed the s S L.r. examination in October. 1979. Shri C. 
Raiendran A fC Plant Operator should be placed in clerical 
c tdre and tim'd nnnronriate wages and allowances hv the Res¬ 
pondent-Bank. This Hon’ble Tribunal may be pleased to 



2319 


[■»m !l—ur>r 3 (ii)] ti tthtct :nf 4, lsas/Wr i4, 1 907 


pass itn award directing the respondent bank to place the 
said Shr. C Rajendran in clerical cadre and to pay wages 
and allowance from ^November, 1979 and render justice. 

5. The Management filed a counter statement resisting the 
claim of the TJnion on the following grounds ; This refe¬ 
rence is not maintainable in law, A workman recruited in a 
paiticuiar grade cannot be automatically placed in different 
grade/higher giade. Shri C. Rajendran was appointed as 
Air Condition Plant Operator at the Local Head Office at 
Madras on 2-11-1977 in the “subordinate” scale of pay and 
not in the clerical grade. The Respondent as a special case 
was paid a sum of Rs. 48 per month as special allowance. 
In 1978 Sri C. Rajendran was paid Rs, 90 per month the 
above special allowance. Sri Rajendran A/C Plant Operator 
at the Madras Local Head Office cannot be consider 
on par with the Air-Condition Plant Operator at the Central 
Office at Bombay whose duties arc different. The educa¬ 
tional qualification and experience are totally d fferent. The 
A/C Plant Opeiator at Central Office perforins onerous duties 
than the A/C Plant Operator at the Madras Local Head 
Office. 

6. An employee who has joined the services of the Bank 
on certain terms and conditions to which he was agreeable 
at ibe time of his entry into the Bank’s service cannot seek 
to alter the same unless and otherw'se the terms and condi¬ 
tions of contract provide for. The duties of an A/C Plant 
Of o a tor at Central Office are different and require better 
educational qualification and experience. The nature of 
duties call for different levels of responsibility and technical 
competence. Sri C. Rajendran is not entitled in law to claim 
highe, grade and special allowance applicable to clerical 
grade on the ground that he has passed S.S.L.C. Examination 
,n Octohcr, 1979. This Hon’ble Tribunal may be pleased 
to dismiss the claim of the Petitioner-Union. 

7. Before this Tribunal. WW 1 was examined and by con¬ 
sent of parties Exs. W-l to W-8 and M-l to M-3 were 
marked. The Management did not lead any oral evidence. 
I heard (he learned counsel for the Union and the learned 
counsel for the Management. 

8. The point for consideration is whether the worker Sri 
C Rajendran, A/C Plant Operator should be paid wages 
and allowances on par with the wages and allowances pay¬ 
able to the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay. 

9. It must be mentioned that though the language of the 
reference describing the dispute suggest that tne claim of 
the workman is that he should be placed in the clerical cadre 
and paid appropriate wages and allowances, the materials 
placed bv the parties in the course of enquiry make it 
abundantly clearly that the claim of the worker is that he 
shoula be pa d w'agcs and allowances, etc., on par with 
the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay, It is dear from the 
document Ex. W-l that initially this worker Sri C. Rajendran 
was appointed as additional Fitter under the Respondent- 
Management. However, the Management considered that the 
duties performed by the additional Fitter are identical or 
similar and therefore the Management chose to re-designate 
this worker who was additional Fitter as A/C Plant Operator. 
This order of re-designation by the Management under Fx. 
W-l was on 28-6-1980. The Management has appomted 
ths worker as A/C Plant Operator by its order dated 
2-11-1977. This order specifies the pay and allowances. 
From this order appointing this worker as A fC Plant Ope¬ 
rator on a particular scale of pay and allowances, the Manage¬ 
ment contends that this worker cannot ask for change of 
emoluments as that would amount to change in the service 
conditions which are governed bv the terms of contract or 
the order of appointment Ex. M-2. I am afraid that th's 
g r ound of objection is not sustainable. The basis of the 
claim of the worker represented by the Union Is that equal 
pay for equal work. 

10. U is the claim of the Petitioner that the worker d : s- 
charKes the mme functions and dut’es in his camicdv as the 
A/C' Plant Opera*or at Madras as the A/C Plant Operator 
discharges the duties arid functions at Bombay. It is further 
aliened that the dories and responsibilities of the A 1C Plain 
Operator at Madras are identical to the duties and respons'- 
fiu tics of the A 'C Plan Operator at Bombay. Therefore 
the basis of the claim is the equal pay for the equal vork 


done. It therefore remains for consideration, whether the 
worker has proved that he has the equal qualification and 
does the rdcntical work and has the same duties and responsi- 
bilit es as that of the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay. 

11. The worker has addressed the letter Ex. W-3 to the 
Management that he has passed S.S.L.C, examination in 
October, 1979 and he is performing exactly the identical 
duties as the A/C Plant Attendant at the Central Office, 
Bombay. He has requested the Management to place him 
on par with his counter-part at Bombay by giving h : m 
the benefit of clerical scale of pay and a special allowance 
of Rs. 200 per month. In reply to this Ex. W-3 letter, 

the Management has written Ex. W-4 stating that 
he was appointed in ihe subordinate cadre and ihe terms 
of appointment cannot be changed at present. The Manage¬ 
ment has advised him to appear for the prornot onal test 
for candidates from the subordinate cadre to the clerical 
cadre if the worker satisfies the eligibility criteria at the 
time of cxaminat on. What is actually asked for by the 
worker is not one of promotion to the clerical cadre. The 
claim is that he should be paid emoluments on par with the 
emoluments paid to his counter-part at Borqbay, namely, 
the. A/C, Plant Attendant. In evidence, this worker WW-1 
has stated that he passed S.S.L.C. in 1979 and be got the 
requis te technical qualification to do the job of A/C. Plant 
Operator. Hr asserted that what all duties are done by 
the A/C Plant Operator at Madras are the same duties 
done by the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay. It was sugges¬ 
ted to ibis WW-1 that the A/C Plant Operators at Bombay 
are appointed on par with clerks. The Management has not 
placed any record to show that the initial appointment of 
A|C Plant Operator at Bombay is on par with clearks 

12. Coming to the functional aspect of the A/C Plant 
Operator at Madras and A|C Plant Operator at Bombay. 
WW-l has stated that they are identical. The Management 
has produced Ex. M-3 in its attempt to show that the duties 
and lesponsibilities of A/C Plant Operator at Bombay arc 
different from the duties and responsibilities of A/C Plant 
Operator at Madras. A close scrutiny of the details men¬ 
tioned in Ex, M-3 will make it clear that the duties per¬ 
formed by the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay are not 
different from the duties performed by the A(C Plant Ope¬ 
rator at Madras. Merely because the A/C Plant at Madras 
is not operated daily or regularly and the A/C Plant at 
Bombay is operated daily that can not make any difference 
In the actual duty to be performed. It may be that two 
members of the staff are ident cally placed and yet the clerk 
working in a particular section has not heavy workload and 
the other cleik placed in another section has not light woik. 
That will not alter the fact that the duties performed by 
both arc identical. The plant at Madras may be medium 
sized afid the plant at Bombay may be big. It is not the 
case of the Management that this worker has not the re¬ 
quisite qualification. It emerges from the pleadings and 
fioni the records that this worker possesses the qualification 
and technical training to work as A/C Plant Operator. In 
fact Ex. M-3 itself makes it clear that after his appoint¬ 
ment he was given training in air-conditioning. Therefore, 
on the ground of qualifications the A/C Plant Operator at 
Bombay cannot be classified as different from the A/C Plant 
Operator at Madras. Merely because there are additional 
accessories or machineries installed at Bombay and those 
additional machineries or accessories are not installed at 
Madras Plant, it cannot be said that the duties performed 
and the responsibil'ties borne by the A/C Plant Operator at 
these two different places are different. It is very clear 
from the evidence that the A/C Plant installed at Bombay 
building has to be operated for 21 floors whereas the AjC 
Plant ins'ailed at Madras Office has to be operated for 7 
floors. This may be the reason for the installation of addi¬ 
tional machineries and accessories at the Bombay Plant and 
for Ihe absence of those additional machineries and accesso¬ 
ries at Madras Plant. Ex. M-3 itself indicates that the drink¬ 
ing water plant at Bombay is maintained and operated bv 
a stpa'ale operator. Therefore the evidence, oral and 
documentary makes it abundantly clear that the duties per¬ 
formed bv tfii- worker at Madias as A 1C Plant Attendant 
are identical to ihe duties performed bv ’he A /C Plant 
Orenfior at Bombay. It s also proved (fiat this worker 
has "ot the requisite qualification after he passed S.S.L C. in 
October, 1979 and the Management has „ot established that 



2320 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MAY 4, 1985/VATSAKHA 14, 1907 


the qualification for A/C Plant Operator at Bombay is 
entirely different or higner than the qualification of this 
worker A/C Plant Operator at Madras. After all the 
Management is the same. The workmen performing identi¬ 
cal duties and discharging identical responsibilities. The 
Management cannot treat them differently because they are 
woiking at different centres. 

13 The Supreme Court in the case reported in 1982—I- 
L.L.J. Page 344 (Randhir Singh vs^ Union of India and 
othcis) wh'le laying down the principle of ‘‘equal pay for 
equal work’’ has observed that where all the things are 
equal, i.e., where all relevant considerations are the same, 
persons holding Identical posts may not be treated differently 
m the matter of their pay merely because they belong to 
different departments. If however, the officers of thg same 
rank perform dis-similar functions and the powers, duties 
and responsibilities of the post held by them vary, then the 
officers cannot be heard to complain of dis-similar pay. As 
has boon already observed in this case, the Management has 
not placed materials to substantiate its defence that the A/C 
Plant Operator at Madras and the A/C Plant Operator at 
Bombay perform dissimilar functions and the powers, duties 
and responsibilities of the A/C Plant Operator at Madras 
are different from the powers, duties and responsibilities of 
the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay. 

14. Adopting the same principle the Madras High Court 
in the case reported in 1984—I—IU page 466 (I 1 . Munisamy 
vs. State of Tamilnadu) has observed that the persons doing 
the same work should be paid the same salary. If it Is not 
so paid, it will be offending the principle of equal pay for 
equal work. His Lordship of the Madras High Court has 
dealing with the case of the senior bails performng the 
duties in the city of Madras and the amins in mofusstl 
courts, Their duties being the same, merely because one 
group does the same work at Madras and the other group 
does the same work in mofussi], the former group cannot 
be paid more amount more and that latter group CBnnot be 
paid less. 

15. Theteforc the worker whose cause is espoused by 
the Union has made out his case that his duties and res¬ 
ponsibilities as A/C Plant Operator at Madras are identical 
to the dut'es and responsibilities of the A/C Plant Operator 
at Bombay that he possesses identical qualification possessed 
by the A/C Plant Operator at Bombay and therefore he is 
entitled to claim pay and other allowances on par with the 
pay and allowances paid to the A/C Plant Operator at 
Bombay. 

16. On that view, I pass an award in favour of the Peti¬ 
tioner holding that he shall be paid pay and allowances on 
par w'th the pay and allowances payable to the A/C Plant 
Operator al Bombay from the date when the dispute was 
referred, namely, 24-5-1983. There will however be no 
order as to costs. 

Dated, this 20th day of March, 1985,

3. It was complained that after their disengagement on 
dilerent occasions the Management recruited, a number of 
freshers in its subordinate staff-cadre on over-looking their

5, It was categorically denied that Balbir Singh, Shivindor

of Setion 25-H for the simple reason that they neither served 
on the permanent cadre strength nor had completed 240 davs 
of service during their entire tennure; what to speak of the 
12 calendar months. 


6. In the same sequence it was propunded that according 
to thc terms of Bipartite Settlement, there was an agreement 
to regularise the services of the temporary staff who had 
worked for 240 days or more but none amongst the petitioner 
had attained that qualification. Moreover only 5 viz. Kaim 
in, Davinder Pal Singh, Ruldu Ram, Rajinder Paul Verma 
and Narinder Singh had applied for being absorbed in the

thc provisions of Section 25-H of the Act for seeking re¬

obviously after the djs-engagement at the petitioners but

was projected on the plea that it was done through approved 
sources like Employmen Exchange; Directorate General Re¬ 
settlement or Soldiers and Sainik Boards etc, 

7. The parties were put to trial on the following issue 
framed over and above the terms of reference ;— 

(1) Whether the Reference is legally bad and incom¬ 
petent 7 

8. In support of their respective versions the parties adduced 
verbal as well as documentary evidence which I have care¬ 
fully persued and heard them at length My issue wise dis¬ 
cussion and findings are as follows : 

ISSUE NO. 1 

9. On behalf of the Management it was aigucd that Bince 
the petitioners’ tennure or service was admittedly of tem¬ 
porary nature, therefore, they could not claim the status of 
“Workmen’’ within thc scheme of Section 2(s) of the Act to 
seek any relief in these proceedings. As a necessary corro- 
lary it was sought to be propounded that the icfcrence itself 
was vojd-ab-initio. 

10. I am not impressed with the effort to knock out the 
petitioners on the mere techanicality of law. On the other 
hand I feel that a bare perusal of Section 2(s) would leave 
no 'manner of doubt about its all embracing and compre¬ 
hensive nature to include even the Casual work force who 
form an inferior category of workers as compared to thc 
Temporary incumbents. For my views I draw support from 
the observations in the cases of Pilot Pen Company (India) 
Pvt. Ltd. Vs. Presiding Officer. Additional Labour Court 
1971(1) LLJ 24l and Crompton Engineering Company 
(Madras) Pvt. Ltd. Vs. Additional Labour Court Madras 
1975(1) LLJ 207. Similarly there is no force in the argument 
that because the employees had served the Bank in a distant 
past, therefore, their claim was stale; after all there is no 
such prescribed limitation in the Act. 

Accordingly I answer the issue against the Management. 

12. Terms of Reference and Relief. 

There is no scope of dispute on any point of fact because 
even according to the pleadings; as per Para No. 1 of the 
Claim Statement and paras No. 1 and 3 of the Written State¬ 
ment, at least seven of the petitioners had served thc 
Respondent Bank at its various branches on some or the 
other occasions for the durations shown against their name 
in para No. 4 of this Award. 

13. Similarly from the Affidavit Ex. W1 and the Certificate 
Ex. W2 it stands proved that the petitioner Nirmal Singh 
had served the Respdt. Bank at their Ludhiana Branch for 
10 days in May and June 1973 where as Affidavit Ex. 20 
and Certificate Ex. 21 establish that the petitioner Shivinder 
Kumar had served the Respdt, Bank at their Miller Ganj 
branch for 27 days from 2-4-73 to 28-4-73. inclusive. And 
it goes without saying that both of hem were examined 
before this Tribunal; they faced the acid test of cross-exami¬ 
nation but their depositions could not be shaken in any 
manner, so much so that the authenticity of the Service 
Certificates produced by them was not even questioned by 
way of any suggestion to the contrary. 



[Part II—Sec,. J(ii)] 


2322 


THE GaZEI fE OF INDIA : MAY 4, 1^85/VAISAKHA 14, 19C7 


14. However there is no evidence on record to assume the 


rendering of service by lire remaining three petitioners, viz; 
hat Dir Singh; Davinderpal Singh, and Baibir Singh shown at 
serial Nos, 1, 4 and 7 respectively, in the Reference—Sche¬ 
dule. 

15. On behalf of the Management it was vehemently argued 
that despite having rendc ed service under it as noted by 
the Tribunal even the aforesaid nine petitioners could not 
be given any preferential ticatment in the matter ol employ¬ 
ment by virtue of Section 23-H of the Act because none of 
them had put in a minimum qualifying service of 240 days 
in 12 calendar months so as to be brought on the Waiting 
list envisaged under Rules 76 and 78 framed under the Act. 

16. Seemingly the aforesaid Rules do contain a rider to 
the effect that a claimant workman should have put in a 
minimum service of 240 days in the preceding 12 calendar 
months; but then, one has also to bcai in mind that the 
Rules are sort of tributory or channel sprouting from the 
Sections and their source. In other words, they are sub-servi- 
ent to the Sections and have no over-riding effect on them. 

17. As a matter of fact the point in issue requires appraise¬ 
ment within the frame work and total scheme of the Statute. 
“Retrenchment” as defined by section 2(oo) is wide enough 
to include the termination of an employee recruited on Tem¬ 
porary, Adhoc or even fixed tenure basis. For my views 

1 draw support from the observations in the cases of Mohan 
Lai Vs. Management of M/s. Bharat Elec, 1981(2) S.L.R. 
ll(SC) and Nawa Shahr, Central Co-operative Bank Ltd. 
Vs. Lab. Court Jullundher (!7r, 1980 Ind. Jour. Reports 
206. 

18. tn the later case His Loidship was dealing with a situ¬ 
ation where a person was employed on Adhoc basis for a 
period of 3 months whose tenure was etxended by another 

2 months n different occasions before the termination with 
the afflux of time. In sort, he had not completed 240 days 
of service in 12 Calendar months. When the industrial dis¬ 
pute arose between the parties, the concerned Labour Court 
allowed his prayer for a prefercmtal treatment in the matter 
of re-employment by virtue of Section 25-H and this proposi¬ 
tion was approved by the Hon* High Court, 1 find no reason 
to walk out of the ratio of the case. 

19. An other angle is that Section 25-F imposes certain 
statutory obligat ons on an Employer towards a ertrenched 
workman who had put in a minimum continuous service of 
one year as defined by Section 25-B. But significantly enough 
no such qualification was required of a workman seeking 
re-employment with the aid of Section 25-H. To put in in 
other words, in its wisdom the Legislature laid down that 
a retrenched workman seeking monetary benefits of retrench¬ 
ment within the purview of Section 25-F, must have put in 
a minimum of one year’s continuous service" whereas no 
such pre-condition was imposed on his attempt to seek pre¬ 
ferential treatment In the matter of re-employment by virtue 
of Section 25-H; and for the obvious reasons I refuse to 
believe that the Legislature lacked the imaginative wisdom to 
appreciate the distinction arising out of the missing phraseo¬ 
logy. Rather for my views I draw support from the ratio 
of Rajbir Singh Vs. State of Haryana 1983 (I) SLR 38 wherein 
{t was laid down that length of service rendered at the 
previous stage was an Irrelevant factor for seeking preferen¬ 
tial treatment In the matter of employment under the same 
employer by virtue of Section 25-H of the Act. 

20. On behalf of the Management it was argued that since 
they were a Nationalised Bank, operating in public sector, there¬ 
fore. they were bound by certain rules of recruitment which 
forbid anybodys’ employment through the back door; and 
that every person seeking employment under them must be 
sponsored hv some or the other approved source like Employ¬ 
ment Exchange or Soldier Saimk Board etc Despite its seem¬ 
ing attraction the submission failed to carry conviction wilh 
me because as a necessary implication of Section 25-J of the 
Act. such Re ruitment Rules must give way to the I eghlallve 
mandate. 

21. Las’ but no< the least, the learned counsel for ihe 
Management submitted that at least two of the petitioners 
viz. Hira Ram and Amarjit Singh had since become over 


age as would be evident fiom the data piovided by them 
in their own documents. Even though the tactual pioposition 
appears to be correct yet we can not loose sight of the 
fact that neither of them was oveiage at the timo of raising 
the damand. So if, for one or the olhei reason, during the 
pendency of proceedings either before the Conciliation machi¬ 
nery or the Tribunal, time ran out ou them for no fault of 
their own, they should not be deprived of the succour of 
social justice as envisaged under the Act. 

22. Thus to sum up my aforesaid discussion on the limited 
available data and the points raised before me, I partiially 
return my Award in favour of the parties. To be precise I 
direct the Management to take appropriate steps for pro¬ 
viding re-employment to the following seven petitioners on 
preferential basis keeping in view the philosophy of Section 
25-H of the Act 

1. Shivinder Kumar. 

2. Kaim Din. 

3. Davinder Pal Singh. 

4. Ruldu Ram. 

5. Rajinder Paul Vcrma. 

6. Vajinder Singh and 

7. Narinder Singh. 

23. Similarly the Management would also endeavour to 
make suitable adjustment of the petitioners Hira Ram and 
Amarjit Singh, and in case it finds any legal or practical 
hurdle in accommodating them, then without creating any 
precedent and purely as a matter of one time compassion it 
would make the offer to one of their dependant family mem¬ 
ber. However, the claim of the remaining 3 petitioners viz. 
Baibir Singh, Davinder Pal Singh and Baibir Singh shown at 
serial Nos. 1, 4 and 7 of Reference schedule stands rejected. 

Dated; 10-4-1985.

AWARD 

The Government of India, Ministry of Labour, vide then 
Order No. L-12012/163/79-D.lbAj dated 15lh Januaiy, 1981, 
constituted an Industrial Tribunal with myself as its Presiding 
Officer, with hcadquarteis at Ahmcdabad, and teferred tne 
following dispute for adjudication to the said Tribunal 

“Whether the action of the management of Bank o. 
Baroda, Regional Office Baroda in terminating the 
services of Sh. H. M. Jadav. sub-staff from 9-9-1978 
is justified 7 If not, to what relief is the said workman 
entitled to 7” 

2. The case of Shri H. M. Jadav (to be referred to as 
“the workman concerned’’) is that he was working as a 
temporary peon at the main office of the Bank of Baroda 
(to be referred to as ‘the Bank”) since 1969; that his salary, 
overtime pay, bonus, ex-gratia pay etc. used to be credited by 
the Bank in his savings account opened in the said Bank; that 
he worked at the said office for a considerable period during 
1972 to 1976 and not only during the period 13-12-74 to 
22-7-75 as stated by the Bank; that thereafer he worked as 
ft peon at the Nagaroada Branch of the Bank since that 
branch was opened. He served there from 6-1-77 to 9-9-78; 
that he worked as a peon at the Nagarwada branch even when 
no permanent peon was posted there. He was paid his dues 
at the Nagarwada Branch on vouchers. He hns further alleged 
that he had applied for the post of a peon in the Bank and 
he was interviewed in the year 1971. He was also called for 
medical examination as late a* in the year 1977. As he was 
not made permanent he hod made a representation after one 
Shri Dilip Rajput (Solanki) a temporary peon at another 
branch was confirmed. According to him, though he had 
passed the medical test, fie was not confirmed that he was 
relieved on 9-9-78 without anv opportunity of service even 
at any other branch of the Bank. This was done without 
following any procedure whatsoever and wjthbut giving any 
reason inspite of his continuous service for long period even 
in permanent vacancy. He has further contended that the Bank 
has not followed the principles of natural justice. His case is 
that he should have been confirmed in the normal course 
inasmuch he had put In a long service and he should have 
been absorbed in anv other branch and confirmed in service. 
According to him. his ’crimination bv the Bank is. therefore, 
illegal and imnroner and deserves to be set aside and he 
should he reinstated as a permanent peon from the dn’e of 
his termination, in anv of the branches of the Bank with all 
consequential benefits. 

3. The Bank has filed its written statement at Ex. 3 and 
has also filed a further written statement at Ex. 6. The Bank 
has also submitted, by way of a preliminary contention, that 
the present dispute relates to reinstatement of the workman 
concerned. However, fro m the order of reference it appears 
clearly that the dispute which has been referred to is a 
collective dispute between the Bank and its workmen. It is 
hence submitted that this dispute having not been raised 
by the All India Sank of Baroda Employees' Union, it de¬ 
serves to be dismissed. It is also contended that no other 
union has raised this dispute and on that ground also it should 
fail. Without prejudice lo the aforesaid contention, it is 
contended by the Bank that even the workman concerned 
did not submit any demand to the Bank for his reinstatement 
and therefore also there cannot be any industrial dispute. 
According to the Bank, the workman concerned was employed 
purely on a temporary basis as a member of the subordinate 
staff during the period 13*12-74 to 22-7-75 intermittently. 
Initially, he was appointed as a peon temporarily and the 
Bank's main office during the period 13-12-74 to 22-7-75, and 
that too intermittently. During the above said period, he 
works for 48 day in the Bank. He was employed at that time 
during Irave vacancies. Then, in the year 1977, he was 
employed at the Bank’s Nagarwada branch wher? he worked 
for 163 davs. Thereafter he was again emnloved in the same 
hraneh in thr yrar_1978 and he worked thee for 78 davR 
In NagTswada branch olio he wa« employed purely on o' 
temporary hnsls to meet with the temporary requirements of 
the Bank. Then, bis services were terminated after office 
hours on 8-9-78, It was also contended that the dim for 


reinstatement is misconceived inasmuch as the workman con¬ 
cerned was employed puiely on a temporary basts and re¬ 
instatement postulates existence of a permanent vacancy. It 
appears from the further wntten statement Ex. 6, that the 
workman concerned was employed purely on a temporary baiss 
with,eiiect front 27-6-72 and during the period 1972 to 1975 he 
worked tor a total period of 214 days intermittently. 1 here- 
alicr, he was engaged as a temporary peon on daily wages 
effective fiotn 6-1-77 at the Nagarwada branch of the Bank 
whero he worked intermittently upto 9-9-78. Avoiding to 
the Bank, during the period 6-1-77 to 9-9-78 he W'orked in 
all for 180 days and his employment was not continuous. 
The say of the workman concerned tbat he was sent for 
medical examination as late as on 5-7-77 has been denied by 
the Bank. According to the Bank, there are various factors 
which are required to be considered before making a work¬ 
man permanent and the case of this workman was such that 
he was not fit to be made permanent. Even otherwise, when 
the workman concerned was appointed, he was told that be 
could not count on permanency and that pending recruitment 
of a suitable candidate for permanent vacancy, he was em¬ 
ployed. Thus, according to the Bank, after appointment of 
a permanent incumbent his services were terminated. It Is 
also the case of the Bank that the workman concerned did 
not work for more than 240 days in any year and hence 
the provisions of Sec, 25F are not attracted. It is further 
submitted that the workman concerned was not appointed 
on probation and therefore the question of his being con¬ 
firmed in service does not arise. It was therefore contended 
that the action taken bv the Bank in the present circum¬ 
stance is legal and justified and the workman concerned Is 
not entitled to any relief. 


4. The workman concerned has examined himself at Ex.8. 
He has deposed that he joined the service of the Bank in the 
year 1969 as a peon in the main office. Initially, he was on 
the salary basis and thereafter he was paid through vouchers. 
From the year 1972 his pay was being credited in his saving 
bank account for which he was issued a pass-book, when he 
was working at the Head Office, he had given an application 
for making him permanent and in lesponse thereof he was 
sent for medical examination on 5-7-77. According to him, 
he had undergone the medical examination also when he was 
working in the Nagarwada branch. He served at Nagarwada 
branch from November, 76 to September, 1978, Then one 
Dilip Solanki was appointed and his (the workman’s) services 
were terminated. When he was working at Head Office, he was 
getting overtime wages and bonus also. The post at Nagarwada 
branch on which he was working was a permanent post and 
there was no other peon in the said branch. He however 
denied that his appointment was on leave vacancies. After 
hjs services were terminated, he had made an application to 
the regional manager for reinstating him. Before terminating 
his services he was not given any notice nor any inquiry held. 
He has further stated that he has passed VII standard. Ac¬ 
cording to him. he had gone for medical examination along 
with several others. In h is cross-examination, lie has stated 
when he was appointed, he was not given any appointment 
letter, but according to him, he was also signing the muster. 
According to him, he was terminated from the main branch 
in November, 1976. According to him. thereafter he was 
taken up in the Nagarwada branch where he worked till 
his services were terminated in September, 1978. He hnd 
admitted that he was not given nny appointment letter and 
that he d'd not make any complaint or grievance in that be¬ 
half. He has also admitted that when he way sent for medical 
examination, he was not given any letter or identity card from 
the Bank to be presented before the doctor He also admitted 
when his services were terminated, he was not given anv 
termination order. He has also very clcarlv admitted that 
he was being employed by the Bank temporarily as and when 
there was work for him As against that, the Bonk has 
examined the Branch Manager at Ex. 20. This witness has 
stated that he was working ns a manager at Nagarwada 
hranrh of the Bank. Th» said branch was opened in the wear 
1976 According to this witness, it was he wffio had taken 
the workman concerned in service as ’’ neon on nurelv tem¬ 
porary basis Hr further strPed that after he was taken up 
in sei vice he was intermit'endv emnloved and he was 
terminated in AeW 1 1478 Aerordinw io *h>‘s witness. he was 
P"ver spnt for medical examination and h" had never riven 
anv nnplieation for being made permanent. In h'r eross-evami- 
nation, this witness has stated that he had not given any
